<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For every fixed <i>ε</i> &gt; 0, we give a deterministic polynomial-time $`(1+2/e+\varepsilon)`$-approximation for finite rational metric <i>k</i>-median with specified candidate facilities, opening at most <i>k</i> facilities. Under $`P\ne NP`$, the infimum approximation factor in this model is therefore $`1+2/e`$.

- **claim-main-approximation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, there is a deterministic algorithm which, given a finite rational metric $k$-median instance with $1\le k\le \|F\|$, returns $S\subseteq F$, $\|S\|\le k$, such that $$\sum_{j\in J}d(j,S)\le \left(1+\frac2e+\varepsilon\right)\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k.$$ Its running time is polynomial in the binary encoding length of the instance. The polynomial may depend on $\varepsilon$.
  - Explanation: For each fixed accuracy there is a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm that opens at most the allowed number of facilities and whose total connection cost is within the stated factor of the optimum.
- **claim-threshold-corollary** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-threshold)): If $P\ne NP$, the infimum of deterministic polynomial-time approximation factors for metric $k$-median with specified candidate facilities is $1+2/e$.
  - Explanation: Combined with the coverage-gap hardness, the main algorithm shows the infimum of achievable polynomial-time factors equals the stated constant when candidate facilities are given and P differs from NP.
- **claim-li-svensson-reduction** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-li-svensson)): Let $\alpha>1$, and suppose $\mathcal A$ is a $c$-additive $\alpha$-approximation algorithm for metric $k$-median. For every $\eta>0$, there is an ordinary $(\alpha+\eta)$-approximation algorithm with running time $n^{O(c/\eta)}$ times the running time of $\mathcal A$. For fixed $\alpha,c,\eta$, this is polynomial overhead.
  - Explanation: An established external reduction turns a constant-additive pseudo-approximation into an ordinary approximation with a small extra loss and polynomial overhead for fixed parameters.
- **claim-grid-preparation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-preparation)): For every $\eta>0$, there is a constant $\Delta_0$ with the following property. Given a finite rational metric instance and feasible rational openings $0\le y_i\le1$ with a nearest-unit assignment of cost $V_j$ for each client $j$, fix any constant even integer $\Delta\ge\Delta_0$ and let $g=1/\Delta$. There is a randomized procedure, running in expected polynomial time on rational inputs, that produces openings $\widetilde y_i\in[0,1]\cap g\mathbb Z$, allowing polynomially many co-located labeled facility pieces, such that $$1\le\sum_i\widetilde y_i\le\sum_i y_i+3g$$ for every output, and the expected nearest-unit assignment cost of every client $j$ is at most $(1+\eta)V_j$. In particular the grid can be required to satisfy any additional positive instance-independent upper bound on $g$.
  - Explanation: Fractional openings can be moved to a fine constant grid with only a small additive increase in opening mass and a small multiplicative increase in each client's expected assignment cost.
- **lem-weighted-dependent-rounding** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding)): Let $q_e\ge0$ be rational counts and let $\beta_e>0$ be rational weights. One can randomly update the counts within the bounds $[\lfloor q_e\rfloor,\lceil q_e\rceil]$, preserving $\sum_e\beta_e q_e$ on every update, until at most one count is fractional. Every first moment is preserved, and the expectation of every product of two distinct counts is at most the product of their initial values. Rounding the possible remaining fractional count with its correct mean preserves these moment assertions. For the resulting integer counts $Q_e$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Q_e)\le q_e,\qquad \mathbb E[Q_eQ_f]\le q_eq_f\quad(e\ne f).$$ The procedure uses linearly many two-coordinate updates.
  - Explanation: Pairwise mean-preserving updates round counts while keeping the weighted sum, all first moments, and an upper bound on distinct pair products.
- **claim-finite-comparison-inequality** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-finite-inequality)): Let $I$ be a finite index set with probability weights $(\pi_i)_{i\in I}$, and let $D_i\ge0$ satisfy $\mathbb E_iD_i=1$. Both $\mathbb E_i$ and $\mathbb E_t$ denote expectation with these weights; in a double expectation the two indices are sampled independently. Fix $0\le x\le1$ and $0<y\le1$, and set $$\Theta=\frac{P(y-x)^2}{y}.$$ Suppose $q_{ti}\in[0,1]$ and $R_i\ge0$ satisfy $$\label{eq:finite-comparison} R_i(1-Pq_{ti})\le D_i+D_t\qquad\text{whenever }q_{ti}<1.$$ No symmetry is assumed for $q$. For any $s_i\in[0,1]$, put $r_i=\mathbb E_tq_{ti}$ and $C_i=1-Ps_i$. Then $$\label{eq:finite-inequality} \begin{aligned} \mathbb E_i\Big[&C_i(1-r_i)R_i +r_i\{mD_i+(2-s_i)Py(D_i+1)\}-C_i(D_i+1)\\ &\hspace{12mm} -\mathbb E_t(1-q_{ti})\bigl(D_t-(1+m+2Py)D_i\bigr)_+\Big] \le2\Theta. \end{aligned}$$ Here $u_+=\max(u,0)$.
  - Explanation: A finite inequality on a probability space bounds the expected drift expression by a small multiple of a quadratic error term, without any symmetry assumption on the directed comparison coefficients.
- **lem-scalar-bounds** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-bounds)): Fix $x\in[0,1]$, put $X=1-x$, and take $0\le w\le X$ and $s\in\{0,1\}$. Use the functions in Equation <a href="#eq:analytic-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:analytic-coefficients">[eq:analytic-coefficients]</a>, and define $$\label{eq:scalar-parameters} \begin{aligned} z&=Pw^2,& b&=(2-s)P(x+w),& a&=A+2Pw,& C&=1-Ps,\\ d_0&=.52,& d_1&=.4,& H_0&=.394-.42X^2+.04X^3,& H_1&=.25,\\ J_s&=CH_s,& k_s&=(1-s)\frac{1-\sqrt{1-P}}{1+\sqrt{1-P}}. \end{aligned}$$ For $q,r\in[0,1]$, set $$\label{eq:scalar-functions} \begin{aligned} \psi(q)&=\frac{1-q}{1-Pq},& \kappa_s(q)&=C\psi(q)-(1-q),\\ K(r)&=C-k_s-br+1.6z,& L(r)&=(C-m-b)r+.4z,\\ M(r)&=(1-r)a+CPr,& G(r)&=M(r)-L(r). \end{aligned}$$ Then the following five bounds hold on the indicated domains: $$\begin{aligned} m+b-\frac{.4z}{r} &\le\frac{C(1-P)}{1-Pr} &&(0<r\le1), \label{eq:scalar-first}\\ L(r)\ge0,\qquad K(r)>0,\qquad K(r) &\ge d_sG(r) &&(0\le r\le1), \label{eq:scalar-linear}\\ \bigl((J_s-b)r+1.6z\bigr)M(r) +(C-k_s-J_sr)L(r) &\ge0 &&(0\le r\le1), \label{eq:scalar-charge}\\ C-k_s+1.6z-bq+.52\bigl(k_s-\kappa_s(q)\bigr) &\ge H_0 &&(0\le q\le1), \label{eq:scalar-type-zero}\\ C-k_s+1.6z-b&\ge.25. \label{eq:scalar-type-one} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Five scalar inequalities on explicit domains supply the positivity and charging facts needed by the finite inequality; they are certified by rational polynomial certificates in the appendix.
- **claim-grid-rounding** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-thm-grid-rounding)): For every fixed $\tau>0$ and all sufficiently large constant integers $\Delta$, put $g=1/\Delta$. Suppose a finite rational metric instance has openings $\widetilde y_i\in[0,1]\cap g\mathbb Z$ of total mass $M\ge1$. For a client $j$, let $V_{j,0}$ be the cost of its nearest unit of this mass. There is a constant $C=C(\tau,\Delta)$ and a randomized algorithm of expected polynomial running time that outputs a set $O$ of facilities with $$\|O\|\le M+C, \qquad \mathbb Ed(j,O)\le(1+2/e+\tau)V_{j,0}\quad\text{for every client }j.$$ All parameters and the additive constant are independent of the instance. In particular, an initial mass at most $k+c_0$, for a fixed constant $c_0$, gives at most $k+c_0+C$ facilities. The deterministic implementation of the resulting total-cost guarantee is given in Section <a href="#sec:derandomization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:derandomization">6</a>.
  - Explanation: A grid solution can be rounded in expected polynomial time to an integral set whose size exceeds the initial mass by an instance-independent constant and whose expected client distances are within the stated factor of the initial nearest-unit costs.
- **lem-constant-additive-count** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rnd-count)): Fix $g,\phi,\ell,T$ as above, with $\ell$ sufficiently small as a function of $\Delta$ and $\phi$. For every fixed $\eta>0$, one can choose $b_*>0$ and a constant $C$ such that the final active mass is at most $M+C$ with probability at least $1-\eta$. The bound holds uniformly over all starting states of total mass $M$.
  - Explanation: With suitable small parameters the final active mass exceeds the starting mass by at most a constant, with high probability, uniformly over starting states.
- **lem-backup-completion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rnd-completion)): Once a copy of $S_0$ is physically removed, an integral facility within distance $5R$ of the client exists and persists to the end. At the end of the process one can retain all integral facilities and open further facilities, using no more than the total final active mass, so that every client is connected within its own distance $5R$.
  - Explanation: Once an initial copy is removed, a persistent integral facility lies within a fixed multiple of the radius, and the final completion connects every client within that multiple while using no more than the final active mass.
- **lem-conditional-batch-drift** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rnd-batch)): For every pre-round history and every realized graph, the physical round, the above alive-set update, and the time advance satisfy $$\label{eq:rnd-batch-drift} \mathbb E[f_{r+1}-f_r\mid\text{history, graph}] \le\ell(\dot f+\mathcal B) +O_\Delta\bigl((\phi+\ell)\ell R\bigr).$$ Here $\mathcal B$ is computed from that graph and the original pre-round alive set. The constants and the required upper bound on $\ell$ are uniform in $b_*$.
  - Explanation: For every realized graph the one-round potential drift is bounded by the ideal first-order expression plus a small error, which makes averaging over graphs legitimate.
- **lem-averaged-ideal-drift** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rnd-average)): Conditional on the pre-round history, the ideal expression satisfies $$\label{eq:rnd-average-drift} \dot f+\mathbb E_{\mathrm{graph}}\mathcal B \le BgV+12\zeta V.$$
  - Explanation: Averaging the ideal generator over the graph and applying the finite comparison inequality bounds the drift by a term proportional to the alive cost.
- **lem-alive-mass-decay** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rnd-survival)): For sufficiently small $\ell$ depending only on $\Delta$, every client’s analysis process satisfies $$\label{eq:rnd-survival} \mathbb Ey_T\le(1-\ell/2)^T\le e^{-\Lambda/2}.$$
  - Explanation: The expected alive mass of original copies decays geometrically over the rounds.
- **lem-affine-support-reduction** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-lem-affine-support)): Let a distribution list have states $s_1,\ldots,s_M$, weights $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_M$, and feature vectors $v(s_i)\in\mathbb Q^d$. There is an exact algorithm that replaces the weights by nonnegative rational weights supported on at most $d+1$ of the same states, preserving $$\sum_{i=1}^M\lambda_i v(s_i).$$ Its running time and output encoding length are polynomial in the encoding length of the given list and feature vectors.
  - Explanation: An exact elimination algorithm shrinks a finite distribution list to few states while preserving the expected feature vector, with polynomial time and encoding length.
- **claim-deterministic-additive-approximation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-pseudo)): For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, there is an instance-independent integer $c\ge0$ and a deterministic polynomial-time algorithm for metric $k$-median that opens at most $k+c$ facilities and has total connection cost at most $$\left(1+\frac2e+\varepsilon\right)\operatorname{LP},$$ where $\operatorname{LP}$ is the optimum value of the assignment relaxation. In particular, its cost is at most the same factor times $\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k$.
  - Explanation: A deterministic polynomial-time algorithm opens at most the budget plus an instance-independent constant and achieves cost within the stated factor of the LP optimum, hence of the integral optimum.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-thm-grid-rounding",
        "exact_quote": "For every fixed $\\tau>0$ and all sufficiently large constant integers $\\Delta$, put $g=1/\\Delta$. Suppose a finite rational metric instance has openings $\\widetilde y_i\\in[0,1]\\cap g\\mathbb Z$ of total mass $M\\ge1$. For a client $j$, let $V_{j,0}$ be the cost of its nearest unit of this mass. There is a constant $C=C(\\tau,\\Delta)$ and a randomized algorithm of expected polynomial running time that outputs a set $O$ of facilities with $$|O|\\le M+C,\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb Ed(j,O)\\le(1+2/e+\\tau)V_{j,0}\\quad\\text{for every client }j.$$ All parameters and the additive constant are independent of the instance. In particular, an initial mass at most $k+c_0$, for a fixed constant $c_0$, gives at most $k+c_0+C$ facilities. The deterministic implementation of the resulting total-cost guarantee is given in Section <a href=\"#sec:derandomization\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sec:derandomization\">6</a>.",

<!-- PM:END pm.claims.v2 -->

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **c-additive alpha-approximation** ([source](#stmt-auto-definition-1)): A $c$-additive $\alpha$-approximation for metric $k$-median returns at most $k+c$ facilities of cost at most $\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k$.
- **distribution list** ([source](#pm-h-35)): A distribution list is a finite list of states, each equipped with a nonnegative rational weight, whose weights sum to one.
- **heavy source** ([source](#pm-h-27)): A source is *heavy* if $g\|E_h\|>B_*$.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-main-ratio | approximation factor of the main algorithm | ≤ | $1+\frac2e+\varepsilon$ | each fixed epsilon |
| q-threshold-infimum | infimum approximation factor | = | $1+2/e$ | deterministic polynomial time |
| q-prep-opening-mass | total grid opening mass | ≤ | $\sum_i y_i+3g$ | every output |
| q-prep-cost-factor | expected nearest-unit cost after preparation | ≤ | $(1+\eta)V_j$ | every client j |
| q-rounding-variance | variance of a rounded count | ≤ | $q_e$ | each coordinate e |
| q-grid-rounding-factor | expected connection distance factor in grid rounding | ≤ | $1+2/e+\tau$ | every client |
| q-grid-rounding-count | number of facilities output by grid rounding | ≤ | $M+C$ | constant C depends only on tau and Delta |
| q-backup-radius | persistent backup distance | ≤ | $5R$ | R is the maximum distance in the client's initial nearest unit |
| q-alive-mass-decay | expected final alive mass | ≤ | $e^{-\Lambda/2}$ | time horizon Lambda |
| q-averaged-drift | averaged ideal drift | ≤ | $BgV+12\zeta V$ | fixed alive set, cap and clock |
| q-affine-support-size | support size after reduction | ≤ | $d+1$ | d is the feature dimension |
| q-pseudo-cost | cost of the additive pseudo-approximation relative to LP | ≤ | $\left(1+\frac2e+\varepsilon\right)\operatorname{LP}$ | c instance-independent |
| q-li-svensson-overhead | running-time factor of the additive-to-ordinary reduction | = | $n^{O(c/\eta)}$ | polynomial for fixed alpha, c, eta |
| q-taper-approximation | rational taper value | ≤ | $\tfrac12$ | fixed rational per round |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-weighted-rounding** [claim](#stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding): Pair updates along a weighted line with mean-zero endpoint choice; coordinates are martingales and pair products are supermartingales. -> integer counts with variance and pair-product bounds
- **step-preparation** [claim](#stmt-thm-preparation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding-2): Form separated representatives and bundles, round deficits together with their suppliers, clip large deficits, and compare costs at far and near scales. -> grid openings with small mass increase and cost inflation
- **step-scalar-bounds** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-bounds): Analytic reductions and exact rational polynomial certificates in the appendix. -> five scalar bounds
- **step-finite-inequality** [claim](#stmt-thm-finite-inequality) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-bounds-2): Bound each positive row by charges to lower-distance rows and show negative rows pay all incoming charges; exchange indices to cancel. -> finite comparison inequality
- **step-count-bound** [claim](#stmt-lem-rnd-count) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rnd-count): Bound the mass-increase statistic in mean and variance, then use a union bound over rounds. -> constant additive mass bound with high probability
- **step-completion** [claim](#stmt-lem-rnd-completion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rnd-completion): Taper radius argument at the first removal, then greedy disjoint neighborhoods by radius. -> backup facility and completion within a fixed multiple of the radius
- **step-batch-drift** [claim](#stmt-lem-rnd-batch) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rnd-batch): Case analysis on relevant actions and on whether forcing removes alive copies; forced case absorbed by free savings. -> conditional drift bound for every realized graph
- **step-average-drift** [claim](#stmt-lem-rnd-average) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rnd-average): Normalize the graph statistics and apply the finite comparison inequality. -> averaged ideal drift bound
- **step-survival** [claim](#stmt-lem-rnd-survival) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rnd-survival): Lower bound the erasure probability of an alive target by a union bound and iterate. -> geometric decay of alive mass
- **step-grid-rounding** [claim](#stmt-thm-grid-rounding) / [proof](#eq:rnd-total-error): Sum the drift over the rounds, add the terminal correction, choose parameters in order, and condition on the mass test. -> expected cost and facility-count guarantee
- **step-affine-support** [claim](#stmt-lem-affine-support) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-affine-support): Repeatedly eliminate a state using an integral affine dependence until the support is small. -> reduced support with same expected features
- **step-pseudo** [claim](#stmt-thm-pseudo) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-pseudo): Chain the preparation list and the physical-rounding list, select cheapest successful outcomes, and allocate the losses. -> deterministic additive approximation
- **step-external-reduction** [claim](#stmt-thm-li-svensson) / [proof](#pm-h-11): Imported established reduction, used as a black box with a rational deletion-threshold implementation. -> ordinary approximation
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-pseudo-2): Apply the pseudo-approximation and then the reduction with small loss, merge copies, and pad to the budget. -> deterministic approximation within the budget
- **step-threshold** [claim](#stmt-cor-threshold) / [proof](#sec:introduction): Coverage-gap reduction gives the lower bound; the main theorem gives the matching upper bound. -> threshold value

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-coverage-cost**: In the explicit metric completion built from a set system, the distances form a metric, and choosing a family of sets that covers c of the m universe elements gives connection cost exactly 3m-2c, so a perfect cover has cost m.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-facility-equals-client**: Transfer the coverage-gap hardness construction to the restricted model in which candidate facilities coincide with the clients. -> The construction uses disjoint facility and client parts, so it does not describe the restricted model, and the paper says its lower-bound question is different.
- **anti-nonmetric-distances**: Use the cost formula and the approximation analysis on distance tables that are not symmetric or violate the triangle inequality. -> The geometric arguments rely on nonnegativity, symmetry and the triangle inequality, so the guarantee and the cost identity are not available for other tables.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The explicit metric completion of a set system in the hardness discussion is a finite construction. The harness builds it, checks the triangle inequality exhaustively and computes the connection cost of a chosen family, which must equal 3m-2c. The approximation algorithm itself is not exercised.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON on stdin with m (universe size), sets (list of lists of element indices), chosen (nonempty list of set indices).
# PRECONDITIONS: elements indexed 0..m-1; chosen indices valid and nonempty.
# OUTPUTS: one line: connection cost, number of clients at distance one, and 1 if the triangle inequality holds on all triples else 0.
# TIME: polynomial in the number of points, cubic for the exhaustive triangle check.
# SPACE: linear in the number of points.
# ARITHMETIC: exact small integers.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and the process exits nonzero.
import sys, json
d = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
m = d['m']
sets = d['sets']
chosen = d['chosen']
n = len(sets)

def dist(a, b):
    if a == b:
        return 0
    if a[0] == b[0]:
        return 2
    f, c = (a, b) if a[0] == 'F' else (b, a)
    return 1 if c[1] in sets[f[1]] else 3

pts = [('F', i) for i in range(n)] + [('C', e) for e in range(m)]
ok = 1
for x in pts:
    for y in pts:
        for z in pts:
            if dist(x, z) > dist(x, y) + dist(y, z):
                ok = 0
cost = 0
cov = 0
for e in range(m):
    dm = min(dist(('F', i), ('C', e)) for i in chosen)
    cost += dm
    cov += (dm == 1)
print(cost, cov, ok)
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper gives a theoretical pipeline with an LP solve, graph rounding and distribution-list compression. It is not an API or a library-level routine. A direct software port would have to assemble these components itself, and the only interface the paper specifies is the reduction step, whose copies are merged back to original candidates. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The iterative rounding is an offline combinatorial analysis device with a constant number of rounds. It is not a time-stepped physical simulation, and the paper claims nothing about game or simulation dynamics. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper supplies no tool or checker for end users. The one reusable engineering statement is the overhead of the external reduction, which is polynomial only for fixed parameters. This is not a tool contract. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | stated | The main theorem is a deterministic approximation algorithm for finite rational metric k-median. It is built from named finite stages: LP solve, grid preparation, a constant number of rounding rounds, expansion of a polynomial-size history tree, and selection of the cheapest feasible output. The final step removes the additive surplus with an external reduction. It is a research-grade construction whose parameters depend on epsilon, so it is adaptable rather than directly implementable. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The distribution list is a proof device for moment-preserving compression, not a reusable structure with update or query operations. The paper gives no operation costs for it as a data structure. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no parallel or hardware-oriented formulation. The rounding procedure is stated only as an expected-polynomial-time randomized algorithm and then a deterministic list-based version. Nothing is said about memory layout, vectorization or throughput. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only hardness statement is an approximation threshold under P not equal to NP for a clustering problem. It gives no cryptographic primitive, reduction to a security assumption, or adversary model. |
| machine_learning | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | Choosing at most k representative centres from a given candidate set to minimise the sum of distances is k-median clustering. The result gives a deterministic factor near 1+2/e when the centre candidates are supplied separately from the data points. It does not address the restriction F=J, and it is a theoretical algorithm, so the use is inspirational. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The problem solved is a cardinality-constrained uncapacitated facility selection that minimises total connection distance. The paper gives the standard assignment LP relaxation, an iterative rounding scheme with a constant additive surplus, and a final reduction to exactly k facilities. It is a research construction, hence adaptable. |
| distributed_systems | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | Placing at most k replicas or caches at candidate sites to minimise total client latency matches the model if the latency is a symmetric metric and servers have no capacity limit. The paper does not discuss protocols or failures. The mapping is by analogy only. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The appendix certifies scalar inequalities with exact rational polynomial minorants and Bernstein coefficients. This is a proof-verification technique specific to this analysis, not a separate engineering domain. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-candidate-facility-model** (hypothesis): The threshold result is stated for instances where the candidate facilities are given as separate input. The paper treats the restricted model where facilities must equal clients as a different question and does not settle it. Blocked use: ['Quoting the threshold as the best possible factor when centres must be chosen among the clients themselves', 'Transferring the coverage-gap lower bound to the restricted model']
- **limit-hardness-assumption** (hypothesis): The matching lower bound is conditional on the complexity assumption in the corollary, and the infimum is not claimed to be attained. The assumption and the facility model are part of the statement. Blocked use: ['Presenting the threshold as unconditional', 'Claiming a polynomial-time algorithm at exactly the endpoint factor']
- **limit-metric-axioms** (hypothesis): All geometric estimates use only the metric axioms. Distance tables that are asymmetric or violate the triangle inequality fall outside the analysis. Blocked use: ['Running the rounding and quoting the guarantee on directed or non-metric latency tables', 'Applying the cost analysis to squared-distance objectives']
- **limit-uncapacitated-relaxation** (hypothesis): The assignment relaxation lets one facility serve many clients, so there is no per-facility capacity. The preparation and rounding steps rely on this. Blocked use: ['Using the algorithm for capacitated server or worker assignment', 'Using it when facilities have different opening costs, since the budget is a plain count']
- **limit-additive-surplus-stage** (edge_case): The rounding stage only gives a constant additive surplus of facilities. The paper says it does not assert the same factor for exactly the budget without the later reduction. Blocked use: ['Treating the intermediate output as a valid answer within the budget', 'Skipping the reduction step and still quoting the factor']
- **limit-fixed-accuracy-constants** (approximation): Grid denominators, round counts and additive allowances are constants that depend on the accuracy, so the polynomial degree depends on it as well. Nothing is claimed for accuracy that varies with the input. Blocked use: ['Reading the result as a uniform polynomial bound for accuracy shrinking with input size', 'Real-time or low-overhead use where the accuracy-dependent exponent matters']
- **limit-total-cost-selection** (formal_scope): The deterministic selection controls the sum of client costs. The chosen solution need not satisfy every client's individual expectation bound. Blocked use: ['Claiming per-client distance guarantees from the deterministic output', 'Using the output where fairness per client is required']
- **limit-exact-rational-arithmetic** (hypothesis): Polynomial running time is argued with exact rational arithmetic and bounded bit lengths. A floating-point port does not inherit the proof of the comparisons and the list compression. Blocked use: ['Replacing exact comparisons by floating point and still citing the guarantee', 'Assuming support size alone gives a polynomial algorithm']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Scope note of family 125 (`lean/docs/125.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Single-exponential recovery and bounded-price strictness for metric $k$-median](../../preprints/Single-Exponential-Recovery-and-Bounded-Price-Strictness-for-Metric-k-Median-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [The approximation threshold for metric $k$-median](../../preprints/The-Approximation-Threshold-for-Metric-k-Median-September-24-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization gives an exact-budget recovery algorithm for metric $k$-median on the stated polynomially bounded integral metrics. Suppose a supplied anchor represents all but logarithmically many comparison clusters by distinct proxies, with total proxy cost at most the comparison cost plus a sufficiently small relative error. The algorithm always opens at most $k$ facilities and, with any requested confidence, attains a $(1+2/e+\varepsilon)$ factor relative to those comparison centers in polynomial time.
>
> The linked final approximation result applies to arbitrary finite rational metrics. For one absolute $\sigma>0$, a polynomial-time fair-bit algorithm always returns a feasible solution, has expected cost at most $(2-\sigma)\mathrm{OPT}$, and attains the same factor with arbitrarily high polynomial confidence.
>
> The formalization gives, for every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, a deterministic polynomial-time $(1+2/e+\varepsilon)$-approximation for metric $k$-median on finite rational metrics with specified candidate facilities. Every output is a nonempty subset of the candidate facilities and opens at most $k$ of them.
>
> Assuming $P\ne NP$, it also proves that the infimum of all polynomial-time approximation factors in this model is exactly $1+2/e$.

Comparator source `MetricKMedian.lean` (2206 bytes, sha256 `52b0567c1c72fddfc813d86788fdd646a8f480687226dd776ec5d4550a6c1b3a`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI




section
section
namespace MetricKMedian

structure Instance where
  n : ℕ
  distance : Fin n → Fin n → ℚ
  clients : Finset (Fin n)
  facilities : Finset (Fin n)
  k : ℕ
  distance_nonneg : ∀ i j, 0 ≤ distance i j
  distance_eq_zero : ∀ i j, distance i j = 0 ↔ i = j
  distance_symm : ∀ i j, distance i j = distance j i
  distance_triangle : ∀ i j h, distance i h ≤ distance i j + distance j h
  k_pos : 1 ≤ k
  k_le : k ≤ facilities.card

def encodeNat (n : ℕ) : List Bool :=
  List.replicate n.bits.length true ++ [false] ++ n.bits

def encodeRat (q : ℚ) : List Bool :=
  [decide (q.num < 0)] ++ encodeNat q.num.natAbs ++ encodeNat q.den

def encodeInstance (I : Instance) : List Bool :=
  encodeNat I.n ++ encodeNat I.k ++
    List.ofFn (fun i : Fin I.n => decide (i ∈ I.clients)) ++
    List.ofFn (fun i : Fin I.n => decide (i ∈ I.facilities)) ++
    (List.ofFn (fun i : Fin I.n =>
      (List.ofFn (fun j : Fin I.n => encodeRat (I.distance i j))).flatten)).flatten

noncomputable def nearest (I : Instance) (j : Fin I.n) (S : Finset (Fin I.n)) : ℝ :=
  if h : S.Nonempty then
    (S.image (fun i => (I.distance j i : ℝ))).min' (h.image _)
  else 0

noncomputable def cost (I : Instance) (S : Finset (Fin I.n)) : ℝ :=
  ∑ j ∈ I.clients, nearest I j S

def feasibleSets (I : Instance) : Finset (Finset (Fin I.n)) :=
  I.facilities.powerset.filter (fun S => S.Nonempty ∧ S.card ≤ I.k)

noncomputable def optimum (I : Instance) : ℝ :=
  if h : (feasibleSets I).Nonempty then
    ((feasibleSets I).image (cost I)).min' (h.image _)
  else 0

def Correct (epsilon : ℝ) (I : Instance) (output : List Bool) : Prop :=
  ∃ S : Finset (Fin I.n), S ⊆ I.facilities ∧ S.Nonempty ∧ S.card ≤ I.k ∧
    output = List.ofFn (fun i : Fin I.n => decide (i ∈ S)) ∧
    cost I S ≤ (1 + 2 / Real.exp 1 + epsilon) * optimum I

theorem main_finite_work : ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε →
    ∃ (algorithm : Instance → List Bool) (P : Turing.TM2ComputableInPolyTime encodeInstance id algorithm),
      (∀ k : P.tm.K, Finite (P.tm.Γ k)) ∧ ∀ I, Correct ε I (algorithm I) := by
  sorry

end MetricKMedian
end
end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For every fixed <i>ε</i> &gt; 0, we give a deterministic polynomial-time $`(1+2/e+\varepsilon)`$-approximation for finite rational metric <i>k</i>-median with specified candidate facilities, opening at most <i>k</i> facilities. Under $`P\ne NP`$, the infimum approximation factor in this model is therefore $`1+2/e`$.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

In metric $k$-median, a finite set $J$ of clients and a finite set $F$ of candidate facilities lie in a common metric space. Given an integer $k$, one chooses at most $k$ facilities to minimize the sum of the distances from clients to their nearest chosen facility. For a nonempty set $S\subseteq F$, write $$d(j,S)=\min_{i\in S}d(j,i),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{cost}}(S)=\sum_{j\in J}d(j,S),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k=\min_{\substack{S\subseteq F\\1\le|S|\le k}}\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}(S).$$ The input distances are rational and encoded in binary, and $1\le k\le |F|$. Both $k$ and the metric are unrestricted. Candidate facilities are specified as part of the input; we do not require $F=J$. The empty-client instance has cost zero and can be handled immediately.

The facility budget is a central difficulty: a solution with small connection cost can be much easier to obtain when extra facilities are allowed. Our approach first permits a constant additive surplus, makes that approximation deterministic, and then removes the surplus using an established reduction.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$, there is a deterministic algorithm which, given a finite rational metric $k$-median instance with $1\le k\le |F|$, returns $S\subseteq F$, $|S|\le k$, such that $$\sum_{j\in J}d(j,S)\le
 \left(1+\frac2e+\varepsilon\right)\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k.$$ Its running time is polynomial in the binary encoding length of the instance. The polynomial may depend on $\varepsilon$.*

</div>

Hardness below $1+2/e$ in this facility model follows from the Max-$k$-Coverage gap: covered clients have distance one and uncovered clients have distance at least three. We use the formulation under $P\ne NP$ recorded by Anand and Lee (Anand and Lee 2024, sec. 1 and 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[AnandLee2024](#ref-AnandLee2024)], based on Feige’s perfect-completeness coverage gap (Feige 1998, Theorem 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Feige1998](#ref-Feige1998)]. For an explicit metric completion, represent sets by facilities and universe elements by a disjoint client set. Give distinct points within either part distance two, and give a facility–client pair distance one for incidence and three otherwise. This is a metric: a same-part edge of length two is bounded by $1+1$, and a cross-part edge of length at most three is bounded by $2+1$. For a universe of size $m$, the connection cost is exactly $3m-2c$ when the chosen sets cover $c$ elements; a perfect cover has cost $m$. Thus a gap between covering every element and covering at most a $1-1/e+\delta$ fraction gives the lower bound $1+2/e-2\delta$, for every fixed positive $\delta$. Together with Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, this proves the following consequence.

<a id="stmt-cor-threshold"></a>
<div id="cor:threshold" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *If $P\ne NP$, the infimum of deterministic polynomial-time approximation factors for metric $k$-median with specified candidate facilities is $1+2/e$.*

</div>

The infimum in Corollary <a href="#cor:threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:threshold">2</a> does not assert attainment of the endpoint factor. The hardness assumption and candidate-facility model are part of the statement. In particular, the corresponding lower-bound question with the restriction $F=J$ is different (Anand and Lee 2024) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[AnandLee2024](#ref-AnandLee2024)].

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context and method

Metric $k$-median has been a testing ground for LP rounding, primal-dual algorithms, and local search. Charikar, Guha, Tardos and Shmoys gave the first constant-factor approximation through LP rounding (Charikar et al. 2002) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[CharikarGuhaTardosShmoys2002](#ref-CharikarGuhaTardosShmoys2002)]. Jain and Vazirani connected the cardinality constraint with metric facility location through Lagrangian relaxation and the primal-dual method (Jain and Vazirani 2001) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[JainVazirani2001](#ref-JainVazirani2001)]. The combinatorial improvements of Charikar and Guha (Charikar and Guha 1999) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[CharikarGuha1999](#ref-CharikarGuha1999)] and the greedy dual-fitting approach of Jain et al. (Jain et al. 2003) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[JainEtAl2003](#ref-JainEtAl2003)] developed this connection further. A different route, the bounded-swap local search of Arya et al. (Arya et al. 2004) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Arya2004](#ref-Arya2004)], achieves factor $3+\varepsilon$.

Li and Svensson (Li and Svensson 2016) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[LiSvensson2016](#ref-LiSvensson2016)] made constant-additive pseudo-approximations a route past the factor-three barrier: a constant number of extra facilities can be removed at an arbitrarily small additional approximation loss. Subsequent bi-point rounding, including the corrected $2.675+\varepsilon$ guarantee of Byrka et al. (Byrka et al. 2017) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[ByrkaEtAl2017](#ref-ByrkaEtAl2017)], refined this approach. Gowda et al. (Gowda et al. 2023) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[GowdaEtAl2023](#ref-GowdaEtAl2023)] obtained $2.613$ in their SODA 2023 version. Cohen-Addad et al. (Cohen-Addad et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[CGLSS2026](#ref-CGLSS2026)] reached $2+\varepsilon$, first announced in 2025. Byrka et al. (Byrka et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[ByrkaEtAl2026](#ref-ByrkaEtAl2026)] subsequently developed a graph-based iterative rounding framework that also gives $2+\varepsilon$ for metric $k$-median and extends to other clustering objectives. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> reaches every fixed factor above the $1+2/e$ hardness boundary in the general candidate-facility model.

Our construction follows the iterative graph framework, with the cost analysis replaced by a directed comparison inequality and a potential that retains some surviving assignments after an opening. The earlier framework’s general transfer theorem uses a parameter at least $2$ in the metric $k$-median case (Byrka et al. 2026, Theorem 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[ByrkaEtAl2026](#ref-ByrkaEtAl2026)]; it does not supply the smaller factor proved here. We establish the grid preparation, physical updates, comparison, and deterministic implementation together.

A separate companion combines anchor recovery with bounded-price payment accounting to give an independent randomized approximation below factor $2$ while respecting the facility budget (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[OpenAIRecovery2026](#ref-OpenAIRecovery2026)].

Two other methodological antecedents are useful to distinguish. Splitting fractional facilities and organizing assignments around separated representatives and bundles are standard LP-rounding techniques; Charikar and Li (Charikar and Li 2012) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CharikarLi2012](#ref-CharikarLi2012)] provide a close predecessor. The pair updates used in our preparation belong to the dependent-rounding approach of Srinivasan (Srinivasan 2001) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Srinivasan2001](#ref-Srinivasan2001)] and Gandhi et al. (Gandhi et al. 2006) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[GandhiEtAl2006](#ref-GandhiEtAl2006)], with weighted variants studied by Byrka et al. (Byrka et al. 2017) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[ByrkaEtAl2017](#ref-ByrkaEtAl2017)]. We prove the particular moment bounds and coupled supplier construction needed here. Likewise, reducing the support of a distribution while matching moments is a standard Carathéodory argument; see Bayer and Teichmann (Bayer and Teichmann 2005) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[BayerTeichmann2005](#ref-BayerTeichmann2005)] for the finite-cubature perspective. Our implementation specifies which moments suffice throughout the physical process and proves the required rational encoding bounds.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof overview

The proof separates the preparation of fractional openings, their iterative rounding, and the removal of randomness and surplus. The intermediate guarantee is measured against the standard assignment LP: its variable $y_i$ is the amount of opening at facility $i$, and each client receives a unit of this opening mass. The LP has total opening mass at most $k$ and cost at most $\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k$.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Preparing a constant grid.

Section <a href="#sec:preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preparation">3</a> replaces the LP openings by multiples of $g=1/\Delta$, where the even integer $\Delta$ depends only on the accuracy. The total mass increases by at most $3g$, and each client’s expected assignment cost increases by an arbitrarily small factor. Separated representative clients define disjoint bundles of nearby opening mass. A bundle’s deficit is the part missing from a unit; its suppliers are the facilities or other bundles holding that part of the representative’s assignment. Rounding deficits together with their suppliers controls both the opening budget and the cost of restoring a full unit. This coupling must allow one supplier to serve many clients, because assignment mass is not a shared capacity.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Rounding the grid.

View every positive grid opening as copies of mass $g$. In each round, a directed graph specifies which copies a source erases when that source’s facility opens. Each target has exactly $\Delta$ incoming sources, chosen with distance-dependent probabilities. Opening and erasing are balanced so that only a constant additional number of facilities is needed. This source-opening and neighbor-erasure framework, including balanced batches and forced large updates, builds on Byrka et al. (Byrka et al. 2026, secs. 3–6) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[ByrkaEtAl2026](#ref-ByrkaEtAl2026)].

The connection-cost analysis needs a different comparison below factor two. For each client, we track surviving copies from its initial assigned unit and a persistent bound on its eventual connection distance. A potential combines their remaining assignment cost with that bound. Its coefficient depends on elapsed time as well as surviving mass. When one of the client’s own copies opens, we continue tracking the cheaper survivors; this produces a subtraction that pays for other possible erasures. The finite inequality in Section <a href="#sec:inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inequality">4</a> captures that payment for arbitrary directed comparison coefficients. Its proof transfers contributions from positive rows to lower-distance rows, whose negative contributions cover them. Appendix <a href="#app:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificates">7</a> proves every scalar estimate by explicit rational polynomial certificates. The geometric substitution and the potential analysis are given in Section <a href="#sec:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rounding">5</a>.

A source erasing many copies is handled by forcing its facility open. Because this choice depends on the graph, its potential change is bounded for each realized graph first. Only then do we average over graphs. This order lets the finite directed inequality control the averaged drift without conditioning on a graph-dependent forcing event.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### A deterministic solution and the exact budget.

The preceding analysis uses randomness, but only specified low-order moments of each local choice enter its bounds. Section <a href="#sec:derandomization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:derandomization">6</a> constructs polynomial-size rational distributions preserving these moments. All physical histories are expanded for a constant number of rounds, and the cheapest output satisfying the additive budget is chosen. The separate rational-arithmetic analysis proves polynomial time in the binary input length. The result is a deterministic approximation opening at most $k+c$ facilities for an accuracy-dependent constant $c$. Li and Svensson’s reduction then removes this surplus with an arbitrarily small additional loss.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Organization.

Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> fixes the model and states the external additive-to-ordinary reduction. Section <a href="#sec:preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preparation">3</a> prepares the openings. Section <a href="#sec:inequality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:inequality">4</a> proves the finite drift inequality, with its scalar certificates in Appendix <a href="#app:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:certificates">7</a>. Section <a href="#sec:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rounding">5</a> gives and analyzes the iterative procedure. Section <a href="#sec:derandomization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:derandomization">6</a> supplies the deterministic implementation and completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Preliminaries and the reduction

We use the client set $J$, candidate set $F$, connection cost $\mathop{\mathrm{cost}}$, and optimum $\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k$ defined in the introduction. Polynomial time includes the binary encodings of all distances. We henceforth assume $J\ne\varnothing$ and $1\le k\le |F|$. This section fixes the fractional representation and states the external reduction that will remove the final constant additive surplus.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The fractional solution

<a id="eq:lp"></a>
We use the standard relaxation $$\label{eq:lp}
\begin{aligned}
 \text{minimize}\quad &\sum_{j\in J}\sum_{i\in F}d(j,i)x_{ij}\\
 \text{subject to}\quad&
 \sum_{i\in F}x_{ij}=1 &&(j\in J),\\
 &0\le x_{ij}\le y_i\le1 &&(i\in F,\ j\in J),\\
 &\sum_{i\in F}y_i\le k.
\end{aligned}$$ Its rational optimum can be found in polynomial time. For fixed openings $y_i$, assign each client to its nearest unit of opening mass, using a fixed order to break ties. If $h_j(t)$ is the distance needed to accumulate mass $t$, put $$V_j=\int_0^1h_j(t)\,dt.$$ Then $L=\sum_jV_j\le\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k$ for an optimal solution of <a href="#eq:lp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lp">[eq:lp]</a>.

As in LP-rounding constructions such as Charikar–Li (Charikar and Li 2012, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[CharikarLi2012](#ref-CharikarLi2012)], it is convenient to split an opening into co-located pieces so that each client’s nearest-unit assignment uses whole pieces. There are only polynomially many needed split points: each client uses at most one partial piece in a fixed distance order. Refine each facility simultaneously at all the resulting cut points. Denote the whole-piece assignment of client $j$ by $F_j$. For a set $U$ of pieces, write $y(U)=\sum_{i\in U}y_i$; thus $y(F_j)=1$ and $V_j=\sum_{i\in F_j}y_id(j,i)$.

Internal pieces at one location are allowed to be distinct, so their distance can be zero. All geometric arguments use only nonnegativity, symmetry, and the triangle inequality. Whenever a procedure returns open pieces, merge them by their original facility identity. Merging preserves every client’s connection cost and cannot increase the number of facilities.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Constant-additive pseudo-approximation

<a id="stmt-auto-definition-1"></a>
<div class="definition">

**Definition 3**. A $c$-additive $\alpha$-approximation for metric $k$-median returns at most $k+c$ facilities of cost at most $\alpha\mathop{\mathrm{OPT}}_k$. The additive allowance $c$ is independent of the input instance.

</div>

We use the following established result.

<a id="stmt-thm-li-svensson"></a>
<div id="thm:li-svensson" class="theorem">

**Theorem 4** (Li–Svensson (Li and Svensson 2016) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[LiSvensson2016](#ref-LiSvensson2016)], Theorem 4 of the 2014 manuscript). *Let $\alpha>1$, and suppose $\mathcal A$ is a $c$-additive $\alpha$-approximation algorithm for metric $k$-median. For every $\eta>0$, there is an ordinary $(\alpha+\eta)$-approximation algorithm with running time $n^{O(c/\eta)}$ times the running time of $\mathcal A$. For fixed $\alpha,c,\eta$, this is polynomial overhead.*

</div>

The theorem permits separate client and candidate sets and applies the pseudo-algorithm to restricted candidate instances. Our construction works on every such instance. We will obtain a deterministic pseudo-algorithm before applying Theorem <a href="#thm:li-svensson" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:li-svensson">4</a>; the external reduction is not being used to convert an expected-cost statement into determinism.

For each fixed accuracy we permit all grid denominators, numbers of rounds, and additive allowances to be constants of any size. They may depend on that accuracy, but not on $k$, the number of input points, their distances, or their bit lengths. Thus it suffices to prove, for each fixed $\tau>0$, a deterministic $c_\tau$-additive $(1+2/e+\tau)$-approximation. Our analysis in fact bounds the pseudo-solution against $L$, the initial fractional cost. This does not assert the same factor for rounding the original relaxation to exactly $k$ facilities: the additive allowance and the subsequent reduction are essential.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Probability and bookkeeping

We use $\mathbb E$, $\mathbb P$, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}$ for expectation, probability, and variance. Every distribution used below has finite support. Randomized procedures first provide the estimates; Section <a href="#sec:derandomization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:derandomization">6</a> replaces their choices by explicit polynomial-size lists.

Two elementary facts will be used repeatedly. First, for a nonnegative random cost $C$ and an event $E$ of probability at least $1-\rho>0$, <a id="eq:conditioning"></a>$$\label{eq:conditioning}
 \mathbb E[C\mid E]\le\frac{\mathbb EC}{1-\rho}.$$ No cost–event independence is required. Second, a finite distribution contains an outcome whose cost is at most its expected cost. These facts allow us to discard budget failures and select a single output by total cost; we never require all individual client expectation bounds to hold simultaneously at that output.

The notation $O_\Delta(\cdot)$ means that its implicit constant may depend on the fixed grid denominator $\Delta$, and on no input parameter. All unqualified constants in the geometric and analytic estimates are absolute, unless an explicit previously fixed parameter is indicated.

<a id="sec:preparation"></a>
### Preparing the openings

We first put the fractional openings on a sufficiently fine constant grid. The use of separated representatives and bundles is motivated by the discretization approach in (Byrka et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[ByrkaEtAl2026](#ref-ByrkaEtAl2026)]. The construction below rounds a bundle’s missing assignment mass together with the facilities that can supply it. This coupling provides both an additive opening bound and a multiplicative connection-cost bound.

Throughout this Section, the client set is nonempty; an instance without clients requires no cost analysis. We start with feasible fractional openings $0\le y_i\le1$. For each client $j$, choose a nearest-unit assignment $x_{ij}$, resolving ties in a fixed order, and write $$V_j=\sum_i d(i,j)x_{ij}.$$ The feasibility constraints are $\sum_i x_{ij}=1$ and $x_{ij}\le y_i$; there is no supply constraint shared by different clients.

<a id="stmt-thm-preparation"></a>
<div id="thm:preparation" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5**. *For every $\eta>0$, there is a constant $\Delta_0$ with the following property. Given a finite rational metric instance and feasible rational openings $0\le y_i\le1$ with a nearest-unit assignment of cost $V_j$ for each client $j$, fix any constant even integer $\Delta\ge\Delta_0$ and let $g=1/\Delta$. There is a randomized procedure, running in expected polynomial time on rational inputs, that produces openings $\widetilde y_i\in[0,1]\cap g\mathbb Z$, allowing polynomially many co-located labeled facility pieces, such that $$1\le\sum_i\widetilde y_i\le\sum_i y_i+3g$$ for every output, and the expected nearest-unit assignment cost of every client $j$ is at most $(1+\eta)V_j$. In particular the grid can be required to satisfy any additional positive instance-independent upper bound on $g$.*

</div>

The expectation in Theorem <a href="#thm:preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:preparation">5</a> is taken after rejecting outcomes that exceed the opening budget. Section <a href="#sec:derandomization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:derandomization">6</a> gives a deterministic implementation that preserves the corresponding bound on the sum of the client costs.

We use the following elementary dependent-rounding observation, in both weighted and unweighted form. The mean-preserving pair update and its product-supermartingale argument follow the standard approach (Srinivasan 2001; Gandhi et al. 2006) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Srinivasan2001](#ref-Srinivasan2001), [GandhiEtAl2006](#ref-GandhiEtAl2006)]; weighted rounding with one residual fraction is also studied in (Byrka et al. 2017, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[ByrkaEtAl2017](#ref-ByrkaEtAl2017)]. We give the proof to specify exactly the moment guarantees used here.

<a id="stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding"></a>
<div id="lem:prep-weighted-rounding" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *Let $q_e\ge0$ be rational counts and let $\beta_e>0$ be rational weights. One can randomly update the counts within the bounds $[\lfloor q_e\rfloor,\lceil q_e\rceil]$, preserving $\sum_e\beta_e q_e$ on every update, until at most one count is fractional. Every first moment is preserved, and the expectation of every product of two distinct counts is at most the product of their initial values. Rounding the possible remaining fractional count with its correct mean preserves these moment assertions. For the resulting integer counts $Q_e$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Q_e)\le q_e,\qquad
 \mathbb E[Q_eQ_f]\le q_eq_f\quad(e\ne f).$$ The procedure uses linearly many two-coordinate updates.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding</summary>

*Proof.* When two coordinates are fractional, move them along the line $$(q_e,q_f)\longmapsto
 (q_e+t,q_f-(\beta_e/\beta_f)t)$$ to one of the two endpoints allowed by their fixed floor and ceiling bounds. Choose the endpoint probabilities so that the conditional mean of $t$ is zero. At least one coordinate becomes integral, and integral coordinates are not updated subsequently. Each coordinate is a martingale. The product of the two updated coordinates is a concave quadratic in $t$; a product involving exactly one updated coordinate has unchanged conditional mean. Thus every distinct-pair product is a supermartingale. Rounding the last fractional coordinate with the correct conditional mean leaves all these products unchanged in expectation. Finally, an integer random variable supported on $\{m,m+1\}$, with mean $m+\theta$, has variance $\theta(1-\theta)\le m+\theta$. This proves the variance assertion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prep-weighted-rounding</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:preparation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:preparation">5</a>.* We first form disjoint bundles containing most of each representative’s assigned mass. We then round their deficits jointly with the openings that supply them, reserving a small amount of budget for clipping large deficits. After rounding inside bundles, every representative can again receive one unit. The final part of the proof compares connection costs at two representative-distance scales and conditions on the budget event.

<a id="eq:prep-parameter-basics"></a>
We will choose positive rational constants in the order $$\delta,\quad M,\quad T_1,\quad\gamma,\quad g,$$ with $\delta$ small, $M$ and then $T_1$ large, and $\gamma$ and then $g$ small. All choices depend only on $\eta$; the last choice means that every sufficiently fine even grid is permitted. During the proof we require $$\label{eq:prep-parameter-basics}
 0<\delta\le\tfrac14,\qquad 0<\gamma\le1,\qquad
 \theta:=\frac1{M\delta}\le\tfrac18,\qquad
 \alpha:=\frac M{\delta T_1}\le\tfrac14.$$ Set $p_0=1-\theta$ and $\beta=M/T_1=\delta\alpha$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### Splitting and representatives.

For each original facility, view its opening $y_i$ as an interval of length $y_i$ and its assignment to client $j$ as the initial subinterval of length $x_{ij}$. Split at the endpoints of these subintervals for all clients. There are at most $|J|+1$ positive-mass pieces per original facility, and every assignment now uses whole pieces. Retain the notation $y_i$ for the mass of a piece, and let $F_j$ be the set of pieces used by client $j$. Thus $$y(F_j)=1,\qquad
 V_j=\sum_{i\in F_j}y_i d(i,j),\qquad
 y(W):=\sum_{i\in W}y_i.$$ Pieces retain the distances of their original facility. They may be treated as distinct candidates throughout the rounding; merging co-located integral openings at the end preserves all connection distances and can only decrease the number of opened facilities.

<a id="eq:prep-representative-properties"></a>
Process the clients in nondecreasing order of $V_j$. Select each remaining client $s$ as a representative and absorb every remaining client $j$ for which $d(j,s)\le MV_j$. Write $\mathcal S$ for the set of representatives. A client $j$ assigned to $s$ satisfies $$\label{eq:prep-representative-properties}
 V_s\le V_j,\qquad d(j,s)\le MV_j.$$ Distinct representatives satisfy $$d(s,t)>M\max(V_s,V_t):$$ indeed, the later one was not absorbed by the earlier one. Let $L_s$ be the distance to a nearest other representative, taking $L_s=\infty$ when $s$ is the only representative. Define $$P_s=\{i\in F_s:d(s,i)\le\delta L_s\},\quad
 p_s=y(P_s),\quad c_s=1-p_s,\quad A_s=F_s\setminus P_s,$$ and let $U$ consist of all pieces outside $\bigcup_s P_s$. For $L_s=\infty$, interpret $P_s=F_s$ and $c_s=0$.

<a id="eq:prep-deficit-small"></a>
The bundles $P_s$ are disjoint. Otherwise a piece in $P_s\cap P_t$ would imply $$d(s,t)\le\delta(L_s+L_t)\le2\delta d(s,t),$$ which is impossible. For finite $L_s$, the mass in $A_s$ has distance at least $\delta L_s$ from $s$, so $$\label{eq:prep-deficit-small}
 \delta L_s c_s\le V_s,\qquad
 0\le c_s\le\theta,\qquad p_s\ge p_0.$$ These bounds also cover $V_s=0$: in that case $c_s=0$.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
###### Supplier stars and signed counts.

For the raw opening count, give every bundle a base level of one. A supplier star couples planned deficits in its target bundles to the mass that will supply them. One planned count unit subtracts mass $g$ from each target bundle and adds mass $g$ at the supplier if it lies in $U$. A bundle supplier incurs no additional opening in this accounting. Thus a star with $d$ targets changes the opening mass by $(1-d)g$ for an outside supplier and by $-dg$ for a bundle supplier.

Make a list $\mathcal E$ of stars; a star $e$ has weight $w_e$, a flag $a_e\in\{0,1\}$ indicating an outside supplier, a target set $D_e\subseteq\mathcal S$, and a specified supplier:

1.  For each $i\in U$, take $w_e=y_i$, $a_e=1$, $D_e=\{s:i\in A_s\}$, and supplier $i$.

2.  For each pair $s,t$ with $y(A_s\cap P_t)>0$, take $w_e=y(A_s\cap P_t)$, $a_e=0$, $D_e=\{s\}$, and supplier $P_t$. Necessarily $t\ne s$.

3.  For each $s$ with $c_s>0$, take $w_e=\gamma c_s$, $a_e=0$, $D_e=\{s\}$, and use as supplier the bundle of a nearest other representative. Such a representative exists because $c_s>0$.

The last stars are called extra stars. The planned count of star $e$ is $q_e=w_e/g$. With $u_e=a_e-|D_e|$, the planned raw opening mass is <a id="eq:prep-raw-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:prep-raw-identity}
 |\mathcal S|+\sum_e u_e w_e
 =\sum_i y_i-\gamma\sum_s c_s.$$ To see this, the flag-one weights sum to $y(U)$, while the total weight targeting $s$ is $(1+\gamma)c_s$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:prep-weighted-rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prep-weighted-rounding">6</a> independently to the positive and negative signs of $u_e$, using weights $|u_e|$. Complete the rounding of the positive class by rounding its possible last fraction with the correct mean. Round the zero-coefficient counts independently between their floor and ceiling. Leave the last fractional count of the negative class, if any, temporarily unrounded. All counts are then integral except possibly this one.

If there is no remaining fraction, the resulting counts are both the nominal counts and the actual activation counts. Otherwise, write the remaining count as $m+\xi$, where $0<\xi<1$, and let its star have $d=|D_e|$ targets. Its nominal count is $m+\operatorname{Bernoulli}(\xi)$. If its flag is one, open $(m+1)g$ at its supplier, irrespective of the nominal coin. Activate $m$ units at each target, and activate one additional unit at every target in a random subset of size $$H\in\{\lfloor d\xi\rfloor,\lceil d\xi\rceil\},
 \qquad \mathbb EH=d\xi,$$ whose membership marginal is $\xi$ at every target. Such a subset is obtained by choosing the indicated size with the correct mean and then taking a uniformly shifted interval of that size in a fixed cyclic ordering of the targets. The nominal coin can be independent of this choice. All other stars use their integral counts for both openings and activations.

<a id="eq:prep-raw-bound"></a>
This modification bounds the raw opening error independently of the number of targets. For the exceptional star its actual signed contribution, in count units, minus its planned contribution is $$a_e(m+1)-dm-H-(a_e-d)(m+\xi)
 =a_e(1-\xi)+d\xi-H<2.$$ A positive coefficient $u_e$ necessarily equals one, so the last rounding in the positive class adds less than one further count unit. The zero class has no signed contribution. If $\widetilde y_i$ denotes the actual opening on $U$, and $Z_s$ denotes the total activated deficit mass at $s$, we therefore have on every outcome $$\label{eq:prep-raw-bound}
 \sum_{i\in U}\widetilde y_i+\sum_s(1-Z_s)
 \le\sum_i y_i-\gamma\sum_s c_s+3g.$$ Moreover, each actual outside opening dominates both its nominal opening and every activation demand placed on it by a single target.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
###### Deficit moments and clipping.

<a id="eq:prep-deficit-moments"></a>
The signed count already controls raw opening mass. The remaining obstacle is that an activated deficit might leave too little mass in its own bundle. We cap that deficit and pay for the added bundle mass with the reserve in Equation <a href="#eq:prep-raw-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-raw-bound">[eq:prep-raw-bound]</a>. For every $s$, let $A_{es}$ be the integer activation count from a star targeting $s$. Then $$\label{eq:prep-deficit-moments}
 \mathbb EZ_s=(1+\gamma)c_s,
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Z_s)\le g(1+\gamma)c_s.$$ Here it matters that the last negative star may depend on the preceding random choices. Condition on the complete count vector immediately before its exceptional rounding. At most one coordinate is fractional, so for distinct $e,f$ targeting $s$, at least one of $A_{es},A_{fs}$ is deterministic under this conditioning. Each has conditional mean equal to its current count. Their conditional product expectation is therefore the product of those two current counts. The distinct-pair product bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:prep-weighted-rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prep-weighted-rounding">6</a> consequently still holds for these activations. Their individual means are $q_e$, and each is supported on $\{\lfloor q_e\rfloor,\lceil q_e\rceil\}$. Thus $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(Z_s)
 \le g^2\sum_{e:s\in D_e}\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(A_{es})
 \le g^2\sum_{e:s\in D_e}q_e
 =g(1+\gamma)c_s,$$ as asserted. The nominal counts also retain the same first and distinct-pair moment bounds.

<a id="eq:prep-budget-probability"></a>
Clip the deficit at one half: $$Z_s^*=\min(Z_s,\tfrac12),\qquad p'_s=1-Z_s^*.$$ Discard any excess activation units. This can be done in whole units because $\Delta$ is even. By Equation <a href="#eq:prep-parameter-basics" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-parameter-basics">[eq:prep-parameter-basics]</a>, $\mu_s:=\mathbb EZ_s\le1/4$. For any random variable $X$ with mean $\mu<b$, $$(X-b)_+\le\frac{(X-\mu)^2}{b-\mu}.$$ Indeed the assertion is immediate when $X\le b$, and otherwise follows by expanding $(X-\mu)^2=(X-b+b-\mu)^2$. Equation <a href="#eq:prep-deficit-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-deficit-moments">[eq:prep-deficit-moments]</a> therefore gives $$\mathbb E(Z_s-\tfrac12)_+\le4g(1+\gamma)c_s.$$ Let $\mathcal G$ be the event that $$\sum_s(Z_s-Z_s^*)\le\gamma\sum_s c_s.$$ If some $c_s$ is positive, Markov’s inequality gives $$\label{eq:prep-budget-probability}
 \mathbb P(\mathcal G)\ge1-\rho,
 \qquad \rho:=\frac{4g(1+\gamma)}\gamma.$$ If all $c_s$ vanish, all activations and discarded amounts are zero, and $\mathcal G$ holds surely. Equations <a href="#eq:prep-raw-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-raw-bound">[eq:prep-raw-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:prep-budget-probability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-budget-probability">[eq:prep-budget-probability]</a> show that a vector with bundle sums $p'_s$ has total opening mass at most $\sum_i y_i+3g$ on $\mathcal G$.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
###### Rounding within bundles and supplying one unit.

Condition on the complete star-rounding outcome, including the retained activations. In each bundle independently, rescale piece $i\in P_s$ to mass $$\overline y_i=y_i\frac{p'_s}{p_s}.$$ Since $y_i\le p_s$ and $1/2\le p'_s\le1$, these masses belong to $[0,1]$. Their sum $p'_s$ is a grid multiple. Apply the unweighted version of Lemma <a href="#lem:prep-weighted-rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prep-weighted-rounding">6</a> to counts $\overline y_i/g$. The preserved sum is integral in count units, so there is no final fraction. This produces grid masses $\widetilde y_i$ with conditional means $\overline y_i$ and nonpositive conditional pair covariances. All masses remain at most one, because one is a grid point. Likewise the outside opening at a piece of original mass $y_i$ is at most $g\lceil y_i/g\rceil\le1$, even under the exceptional correction.

Every representative $s$ can now receive one unit: take its entire bundle mass $p'_s$ and supply its retained deficit $Z_s^*$ using the retained activations. Each outside supplier has enough mass for its activation demand at $s$. Any bundle supplier is different from $P_s$. The total demand of $s$ on any one such bundle is at most its entire retained deficit, hence at most $1/2$, while every supplying bundle has mass at least $1/2$. Allocate this demand greedily over its pieces. The bundles and $U$ are disjoint, and different clients have separate assignment constraints. This proves feasibility for each client individually, even before conditioning on $\mathcal G$. It also shows that the total opening mass is at least one.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### Cost comparison at a distant representative scale.

Fix a client $j$ and its representative $s$, and put $D=d(j,s)$. Let $R_s=\max_{i\in F_s}d(s,i)$, and define the radius $$K_s=\begin{cases}L_s,&c_s>0,\\R_s,&c_s=0.\end{cases}$$ If $V_j=0$, Equation <a href="#eq:prep-representative-properties" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-representative-properties">[eq:prep-representative-properties]</a> gives $D=V_s=0$. All pieces of $F_s$ then have distance zero from $j$, $c_s=0$, and $Z_s=0$ surely. The rounded bundle $P_s=F_s$ still has one unit at distance zero. Hence the new cost is identically zero. We may therefore assume $V_j>0$ in the remaining cost analysis.

<a id="eq:prep-far-exchange"></a>
Suppose first that $K_s>T_1V_j$. We have $R_s\ge\delta K_s$: this is immediate if $c_s=0$, and otherwise some assigned mass lies beyond $\delta L_s$. Consequently $D/R_s\le\alpha<1$. This controls the cost of exchanging the portions of $F_j$ and $F_s$ that are not shared. Leave the shared pieces matched identically. The masses of $F_s\setminus F_j$ and $F_j\setminus F_s$ are equal. Each piece in the former set is at distance at most $R_s+D$ from $j$. Each piece in the latter set is at distance at least $R_s-D$ from $j$, because the nearest-unit assignment at $s$ uses all closer mass before stopping at radius $R_s$. This argument remains valid at ties. Comparing the two residual costs shows that using the old assignment $F_s$ for client $j$ has cost at most $$\label{eq:prep-far-exchange}
 C_j(F_s):=\sum_{i\in F_s}y_i d(j,i)
 \le\frac{1+\alpha}{1-\alpha}V_j.$$

Serve $j$ with the feasible assignment just constructed for $s$. For each $i\in P_s$, $$\mathbb E\widetilde y_i
 =y_i\mathbb E[p'_s]/p_s\le y_i/p_s.$$ For an ordinary star, the expected retained activation mass is at most its original weight. Thus outside suppliers do not increase their expected cost charge. It remains to compare arbitrary new supply inside a bundle $P_t$ with the old pieces in $A_s\cap P_t$. This case only arises when $c_s>0$. Then $D\le\beta L_s$, and for any old supplier piece $i\in P_t$, $$d(j,i)\ge d(s,t)-D-\delta L_t
 \ge(1-\beta-\delta)d(s,t).$$ Any two pieces of $P_t$ are at distance at most $2\delta L_t$. Because $L_t\le d(s,t)$ and $1-\beta-\delta>0$, every possible new supplier piece has distance to $j$ at most $$\kappa\,d(j,i),\qquad
 \kappa:=1+\frac{2\delta}{1-\beta-\delta}.$$ The comparison holds for every old piece, so it also holds relative to the weighted mean distance of the old pieces aggregated by that star. Multiplying by its expected retained activation mass bounds its expected cost by $\kappa$ times its old cost contribution.

<a id="eq:prep-far-factor"></a>
Finally, for the extra star at $s$, its supplier $P_t$ satisfies $d(s,t)=L_s$ and $L_t\le L_s$. Every supply position is therefore at distance at most $(\beta+1+\delta)L_s$ from $j$. By Equation <a href="#eq:prep-deficit-small" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-deficit-small">[eq:prep-deficit-small]</a>, its expected cost is at most $$(\beta+1+\delta)\gamma c_s L_s
 \le\frac{(\beta+1+\delta)\gamma}{\delta}V_j.$$ There is no extra star when $c_s=0$. Combining these bounds with Equation <a href="#eq:prep-far-exchange" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-far-exchange">[eq:prep-far-exchange]</a>, the unconditional expected nearest-unit cost in this case is at most $B_{\mathrm{far}}V_j$, where $$\label{eq:prep-far-factor}
 B_{\mathrm{far}}
 :=\max\{p_0^{-1},\kappa\}\frac{1+\alpha}{1-\alpha}
   +\frac{(\beta+1+\delta)\gamma}{\delta}.$$

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### Cost comparison at a nearby representative scale.

Now suppose $K_s\le T_1V_j$. Consider the mass on the old set $F_j$, using nominal rounded masses on $U$ and actual rounded masses in the bundles. This mass is available in the actual opening vector. Its unconditional expected cost is at most $V_j/p_0$: nominal outside openings have mean $y_i$, and a bundle piece has expected mass at most $y_i/p_s\le y_i/p_0$.

<a id="eq:prep-outside-noise"></a>
<a id="eq:prep-conditional-noise"></a>
We bound the expected shortfall of this available mass from one. Write $M_U$ for its nominal mass on $U\cap F_j$. The first and pair moments of the nominal counts imply $$\label{eq:prep-outside-noise}
 \mathbb EM_U=y(U\cap F_j),\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(M_U)\le g\,y(U\cap F_j)\le g.$$ For a fixed complete star outcome, put $$a_t=y(F_j\cap P_t),\qquad
 \overline M_P=\sum_t a_t p'_t/p_t,$$ and write $M_P$ for the actual rounded mass on these bundle pieces. Conditional on the star outcome, bundle rounding has nonpositive pair covariances within each bundle and is independent between bundles. The variance of a single grid-rounded mass of conditional mean $\overline y_i$ is at most $g\overline y_i$. Therefore $$\label{eq:prep-conditional-noise}
 \mathbb E[M_P\mid\text{star outcome}]=\overline M_P,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(M_P\mid\text{star outcome})
 \le g\overline M_P\le g/p_0.$$

<a id="eq:prep-shortfall"></a>
Clipping at $1/2$ cannot increase distance from $c_t\le1/2$, so Equation <a href="#eq:prep-deficit-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-deficit-moments">[eq:prep-deficit-moments]</a> gives <a id="eq:prep-rescaling-noise"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E|p'_t-p_t|
 &=\mathbb E|c_t-Z_t^*|\notag\\
 &\le\mathbb E|c_t-Z_t|
 \le\gamma c_t+\sqrt{g(1+\gamma)c_t}.
 \label{eq:prep-rescaling-noise}
\end{aligned}$$ Since $\sum_t a_t\le1$, it follows that $$\mathbb E\left|\overline M_P-\sum_t a_t\right|
 \le\sum_t\frac{a_t}{p_t}\mathbb E|p'_t-p_t|
 \le\frac{\gamma+\sqrt{g(1+\gamma)}}{p_0}.$$ Use the identity $y(U\cap F_j)+\sum_t a_t=1$, the triangle inequality, and the second-moment bounds in Equations <a href="#eq:prep-outside-noise" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-outside-noise">[eq:prep-outside-noise]</a>–<a href="#eq:prep-conditional-noise" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-conditional-noise">[eq:prep-conditional-noise]</a>. The expected shortfall is at most $$\label{eq:prep-shortfall}
 \mathbb E(1-M_U-M_P)_+\le H,
 \qquad
 H:=\sqrt g+\sqrt{g/p_0}
       +\frac{\gamma+\sqrt{g(1+\gamma)}}{p_0}.$$ In particular no independence between outside counts and rescaled bundle masses is being assumed.

<a id="eq:prep-near-factor"></a>
Trim any surplus mass on $F_j$, which cannot increase its cost. To fill a remaining shortfall, there is always one unit of available opening within distance $$QV_j,\qquad Q:=M+(1+\delta)T_1,$$ from $j$. Indeed, if $c_s=0$, there are no activations at $s$ and $P_s$ has exactly one unit, all at distance at most $D+R_s\le(M+T_1)V_j$. If $c_s>0$, use $P_s$ and the bundle $P_t$ of a nearest other representative. They are disjoint and each has at least half a unit. Their supply lies within distance $D+(1+\delta)L_s\le QV_j$. This supply can fill the shortfall even if some of it is already used: if the current assignment has mass $1-q$, a ball containing at least one opening unit contains at least $q$ unused units. Allocate those units to finish the assignment. By Equation <a href="#eq:prep-shortfall" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-shortfall">[eq:prep-shortfall]</a>, the unconditional expected nearest-unit cost is at most $B_{\mathrm{near}}V_j$, where $$\label{eq:prep-near-factor}
 B_{\mathrm{near}}:=p_0^{-1}+QH.$$

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### Choosing parameters and enforcing the budget.

Equations <a href="#eq:prep-far-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-far-factor">[eq:prep-far-factor]</a> and <a href="#eq:prep-near-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-near-factor">[eq:prep-near-factor]</a> give explicit bounds that approach one in the stated order of parameter choices. More precisely, first choose $\delta$ small to make the limiting value of $\kappa$ close to one. Next choose $M$ large so that $p_0^{-1}$ is close to one, and then choose $T_1$ large so that $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are small. With these constants fixed, choose $\gamma$ small enough to control both the last term of $B_{\mathrm{far}}$ and the term $Q\gamma/p_0$ in $B_{\mathrm{near}}$. Finally choose $g$ small enough to control all square-root terms and $\rho$ in Equation <a href="#eq:prep-budget-probability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:prep-budget-probability">[eq:prep-budget-probability]</a>. All constants may be rational, and all sufficiently small $g=1/\Delta$ with even $\Delta$ satisfy the resulting strict bounds. In particular, arrange $$\rho<\tfrac12,\qquad
 \frac{\max\{B_{\mathrm{far}},B_{\mathrm{near}}\}}{1-\rho}
 \le1+\eta.$$

Reject a star-rounding outcome outside $\mathcal G$ and repeat. For the purpose of the preceding unconditional analysis, the subsequent bundle rounding can be imagined on every outcome. Let $\widetilde V_j$ denote the resulting nearest-unit cost. Its nonnegativity, rather than any independence from $\mathcal G$, gives <a id="eq:prep-cost-final"></a>$$\label{eq:prep-cost-final}
 \mathbb E[\widetilde V_j\mid\mathcal G]
 \le\frac{\mathbb E\widetilde V_j}{\mathbb P(\mathcal G)}
 \le(1+\eta)V_j.$$ The already established supply and opening bounds hold on every accepted outcome.

The split representation, star list, and number of pair updates are polynomial in the input size. Pair updates and subset choices use rational probabilities and rational linear operations. Their encodings have polynomial bit length: each weighted update introduces only ratios of integer weights bounded by $|\mathcal S|$, and there are polynomially many updates; the subsequent rescalings divide by the rational masses $p_s$. Exact rational sampling has expected polynomial running time. The acceptance probability is at least one half, so rejection multiplies the expected running time by at most two. This proves the theorem. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:inequality"></a>
### A finite inequality for the drift

The cost analysis uses an inequality on a finite probability space. Its comparison matrix need not be symmetric. The proof allows a positive contribution at one index only by charging indices with smaller values; the negative contributions at those indices pay the entire charge.

The statement below is independent of the rounding construction. Section <a href="#sec:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rounding">5</a> will apply it to normalized client distances and directed edge probabilities; Equation <a href="#eq:rnd-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="eq:rnd-normalization">[eq:rnd-normalization]</a> records that substitution. We prove the finite statement first so that the geometric analysis can use it as a single input.

<a id="eq:analytic-coefficients"></a>
For $0\le x\le1$, define $$\label{eq:analytic-coefficients}
\begin{aligned}
 A(x)&=1+2\int_0^1(1-e^{-tx})\,dt,&
 P(x)&=\int_0^1te^{-tx}\,dt,\\
 B(x)&=2P(x),& m(x)&=A(x)-1-2xP(x).
\end{aligned}$$ We suppress the argument $x$ when it is fixed. Differentiation under the integral gives $A'=B$ and $A''(x)=-2\int_0^1t^2e^{-tx}\,dt<0$.
